Substituting the last display in the sum of (
15
) over
\sigma_{\bullet}
, we get
\mathtt{mATRC}_{K}
.
∎

4.3.
Properties of the modified ATRC

In this section, we will derive basic properties of the modified ATRC measure that will be instrumental in its analysis in Section
8
and in the proof of Theorem
1.3
.
We continue in the setting of the previous section.

Positive association.

The motivation for sampling the modified ATRC only on
\bar{E}=E\cup E_{\mathrm{b}}^{+}
(rather than on
E\cup E_{\mathrm{b}}
) is that this (marginal) distribution satisfies (
strong-FKG
).
This allows to “sandwich” the associated modified ATRC measure between unmodified ATRC measures and deduce convergence to the unique infinite-volume
Gibbs measure
; see Sections
3
and
5
.

Lemma 4.6
.

The measure
\mathtt{mATRC}_{K}
satisfies (
strong-FKG
).

Proof.

Recall that
\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}=e^{\lambda}
with
\lambda>0
.
We verify the Holley criterion
[
Hol74
]
, see also
[
GHM01
, Section 4]
.
To shorten notation, given
a,b\in\{0,1\}^{\bar{E}}
with
a\subseteq b
and
a=b
on
E_{b}^{+}
, we write
\mathtt{mATRC}_{K}(a_{e},b_{e}\,|\,a_{e^{c}},b_{e^{c}})
for the conditional probability

\mathtt{mATRC}_{K}\big((\omega_{\tau}(e),\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}(e))=(a_{e},b_{e})\,\big|\,(\omega_{\tau},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})=(a,b)\text{ on }(\bar{E}\setminus\{e\}\big).

Case 1:
e=ij\in E
.

Denote by
C_{i}^{a},C_{j}^{a}
respectively the clusters of
a_{e^{c}}
containing
i,j
, and by
\widetilde{C}_{i}^{b},\widetilde{C}_{j}^{b}
the clusters of
b_{e^{c}}\cup E_{\mathrm{b}}^{+}
containing
i,j
. Observe that
\widetilde{C}_{i}^{b}\neq\widetilde{C}_{j}^{b}
implies that
\widetilde{C}_{i}^{b}\subseteq\Lambda
or
\widetilde{C}_{j}^{b}\subseteq\Lambda
.
Then, for any
a,b\in\{0,1\}^{E\cup E_{\mathrm{b}}^{+}}
with
a\subseteq b
and
a=b
on
E_{b}^{+}
,

(16)

\mathtt{mATRC}_{K}(a_{e},b_{e}\,|\,a_{e^{c}},b_{e^{c}})=\big(2^{a_{e}}\tfrac{\mathbf{c}}{\mathbf{c}+1}\big)^{\mathds{1}_{C_{i}^{a}=C_{j}^{a}}}(\mathbf{c}-2)^{b_{e}-a_{e}}\tfrac{f(b)^{b_{e}}}{1+(\mathbf{c}-1)f(b)},

where

f(b)=\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{C}_{i}^{b}=\widetilde{C}_{j}^{b}}+\alpha\big(\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{C}_{i}^{b}\neq\widetilde{C}_{j}^{b}\not\subseteq\Lambda}+\mathds{1}_{\Lambda\not\supseteq\widetilde{C}_{i}^{b}\neq\widetilde{C}_{j}^{b}}\big)+\beta\mathds{1}_{\Lambda\supseteq\widetilde{C}_{i}^{b}\neq\widetilde{C}_{j}^{b}\subseteq\Lambda}

and

\alpha=\frac{e^{\lambda I(\widetilde{C}_{i}^{b})}}{1+e^{\lambda I(\widetilde{C}_{i}^{b})}},\qquad\beta=\frac{1+e^{\lambda I(\widetilde{C}_{i}^{b})+\lambda I(\widetilde{C}_{j}^{b})}}{(1+e^{\lambda I(\widetilde{C}_{i}^{b})})(1+e^{\lambda I(\widetilde{C}_{j}^{b})})}.

Note first that
\mathbf{c}\geq 1
(in fact
\mathbf{c}>2
) and
\mathds{1}_{C_{i}^{a}=C_{j}^{a}}
is increasing in
a
. Moreover,
\beta\leq\alpha\leq 1
and both
\alpha
and
\beta
are increasing in
I(\widetilde{C}_{i}^{b})
and
I(\widetilde{C}_{j}^{b})
and hence in
b
. Therefore
f\geq 1
is increasing in
b
. This implies that
\mathtt{mATRC}_{K}(1,1\,|\,a_{e^{c}},b_{e^{c}})
is increasing in
a,b
and
\mathtt{mATRC}_{K}(0,0\,|\,a_{e^{c}},b_{e^{c}})
is decreasing in
a,b
.

Case 2:
e=\{i,j\}\in E_{b}^{+}
.
If
a_{e}=b_{e}
, one has

(17)

\mathtt{mATRC}_{K}(a_{e},b_{e}\,|\,a_{e^{c}},b_{e^{c}})=\big(2^{a_{e}}\tfrac{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}}{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}+1}\big)^{\mathds{1}_{C_{i}^{a}=C_{j}^{a}}}\Big(\tfrac{f(b)}{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}-1}\Big)^{a_{e}}\tfrac{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}-1}{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}-1+f(b)},

where
f(b)
is as above.
Recall that
\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}=e^{\lambda}>1
and that
\mathds{1}_{C_{i}^{a}=C_{j}^{a}}
and
f
are increasing in
a
and
b
, respectively. This implies that
\mathtt{mATRC}_{K}(1,1\,|\,a_{e^{c}},b_{e^{c}})
is increasing in
a,b
, and hence
\mathtt{mATRC}_{K}(0,0\,|\,a_{e^{c}},b_{e^{c}})
is decreasing in
a,b
, and the proof is complete.
∎

Finite energy.

The explicit expressions (
16
)-(
17
) of the conditional probabilities readily imply that
\mathtt{mATRC}_{K}
satisfies the finite-energy property:

Lemma 4.7
.

The exists a constant
c>0
such that, for any
e\in E
and any
a,b\in\{0,1\}^{E\cup E_{\mathrm{b}}^{+}}
with
a\subseteq b
and
b\setminus a\subseteq E
,

\mathtt{mATRC}_{K}\big((\omega_{\tau}(e),\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}(e))=(a_{e},b_{e})\,\big|\,(\omega_{\tau},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})=(a,b)\text{ on }\bar{E}\setminus\{e\}\big)>c.

The statement also holds for
e\in E_{\mathrm{b}}^{+}
, provided that
a_{e}=b_{e}
.

Decoupling property.

The following decoupling property (and analogues of it), which also holds for the standard ATRC measures, will be crucial in the derivation of mixing properties in Sections
5
-
6
and the invariance principle in Section
8
.

Let us first introduce some terminology.

Definition 4.8
.

We identify circuits in
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbb{L}_{\circ}
with their sets of edges as well as with their planar embedding given by the union of line-segments between consecutive vertices.
Given a simple circuit
\gamma
in
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
or
\mathbb{L}_{\circ}
, we say that a subset
R\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is
within

\gamma
if it is contained in the topological closure of the bounded connected component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\gamma
. In this case,
\gamma
is said to
surround

R
, or
R
is surrounded by
\gamma
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
(\omega_{\tau},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})
be distributed according to
\mathtt{mATRC}_{K}
. Let
F_{1}\subset F_{2}\subset\bar{E}
, and let
a,b\in\{0,1\}^{F_{2}\setminus F_{1}}
with
a\subseteq b
and
b\setminus a\subseteq E
be such that there exist circuits in
a^{*}
and in
b
that both surround the edges in
F_{1}
. Then, conditionally on
(\omega_{\tau}|_{F_{2}\setminus F_{1}},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}|_{F_{2}\setminus F_{1}})=(a,b)
, the restrictions

(\omega_{\tau}|_{F_{1}},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}|_{F_{1}})\quad\text{and}\quad(\omega_{\tau}|_{\bar{E}\setminus F_{2}},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}|_{\bar{E}\setminus F_{2}})

are independent.

Proof.

Take any
a_{1},b_{1}\in\{0,1\}^{F_{1}}
and
a_{2},b_{2}\in\{0,1\}^{\bar{E}\setminus F_{2}}
, and write

\bar{a}=a_{1}\sqcup a\sqcup a_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{b}=b_{1}\sqcup b\sqcup b_{2}.

By Definition
4.3
,

(18)

\displaystyle\mathtt{mATRC}_{K}\big((\bar{a},\bar{b})\,\big|\,(\omega_{\tau}|_{F_{2}\setminus F_{1}},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}|_{F_{2}\setminus F_{1}})=(a,b)\big)

\displaystyle\propto\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{2}\mathds{1}_{a_{i}\subseteq b_{i}}\mathds{1}_{b_{i}\setminus a_{i}\subseteq E}2^{|a_{i}\cap E|}\big(\tfrac{2}{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}-1}\big)^{|a_{i}\cap E_{\mathrm{b}}^{+}|}(\mathbf{c}-2)^{|b_{i}\setminus a_{i}|}\bigg)2^{\kappa_{K}(\bar{a})}\prod_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{cl}_{\Lambda}(\bar{b})}\big(\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Lambda}+\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}^{I(\mathcal{C})}\big),

where the constant of proportionality depends on
a,b
.

Let
\gamma^{\prime}\subset a^{*}\subset\mathbb{E}^{\circ}
be a circuit that surrounds
F_{1}
.
Let
E_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{in}}\subset\bar{E}
be the set of edges within
\gamma^{\prime}
, and set
E_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{ex}}=\bar{E}\setminus(E_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{in}}\cup*\gamma^{\prime})
. For
\#\in\{\mathrm{in},\mathrm{ex}\}
, let
K_{a}^{\#}
be the graph obtained from
(\mathbb{V}_{E_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\#}},E_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\#})
by identifying endpoints of edges in
a
. As the circuit
\gamma^{\prime}
is open in
a^{*}
and separates
K_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}
from
K_{a}^{\mathrm{ex}}
, we clearly have

\kappa_{K}(\bar{a})=\kappa_{K_{a}^{\mathrm{in}}}(a_{1})+\kappa_{K_{a}^{\mathrm{ex}}}(a_{2}).

Similarly, let
\gamma\subset b\subset\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}
be a circuit that surrounds
F_{1}
. Let
E_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{in}}\subset\bar{E}
be the set of edges within
\gamma
, and set
E_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{ex}}=\bar{E}\setminus E_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{in}}
. For
\#\in\{\mathrm{in},\mathrm{ex}\}
, define
K_{b}^{\#}
as the graph obtained from
(\mathbb{V}_{E_{\gamma}^{\#}},E_{\gamma}^{\#})
by identifying endpoints of edges in
b
.
Let
\mathrm{cl}_{K_{b}^{\mathrm{in}}}(b_{1})
and
\mathrm{cl}_{K_{b}^{\mathrm{ex}}}(b_{2})
be the sets of clusters of
b_{1}
in
K_{b}^{\mathrm{in}}
and
b_{2}
in
K_{b}^{\mathrm{ex}}
, respectively.
Then, every cluster in
\mathrm{cl}_{\Lambda}(\bar{b})
that does not contain
\mathbb{V}_{\gamma}
belongs to either
\mathrm{cl}_{K_{b}^{\mathrm{in}}}(b_{1})
or
\mathrm{cl}_{K_{b}^{\mathrm{ex}}}(b_{2})
.
Moreover, every cluster
\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{cl}_{K_{b}^{\mathrm{in}}}(b_{1})
except that of the vertex corresponding to
\gamma
satisfies
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Lambda
and
I(\mathcal{C})=0
. Therefore,

\prod_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{cl}_{\Lambda}(\bar{b})}\big(\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Lambda}+\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}^{I(\mathcal{C})}\big)=2^{|\mathrm{cl}_{K_{b}^{\mathrm{in}}}(b_{1})|-1}\prod_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{cl}_{K_{b}^{\mathrm{ex}}}(b_{2})}\big(\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Lambda}+\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}^{I(\mathcal{C})}\big).

Therefore, the expression in (
18
) factorises, and the proof is complete.
∎

4.4.
Interface in the modified ATRC

Recall the coupling measure
\Psi^{1/0}
of the six-vertex spin random variable
(\sigma_{\bullet},\sigma_{\circ})\sim\mathtt{Spin}_{\mathcal{D}}^{+,+-}
and the modified ATRC pair
(\omega_{\tau},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})\sim\mathtt{mATRC}_{K}(\cdot\,|\,v_{L}\xleftrightarrow{\omega_{\tau}}v_{R})
(Lemmata
4.2
and
4.4
).

Since
\sigma_{\circ}
is constant on edges in
\omega_{\tau}^{*}
, the Dobrushin boundary conditions (
\sigma_{\circ}\in\Sigma_{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{+-}
) impose the existence of a path in
\omega_{\tau}
that connects
v_{L}
and
v_{R}
.

Definition 4.10
.

Denote by
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{v_{L}}\subseteq\bar{V}
the cluster of
v_{L}
in
\omega_{\tau}
; see Fig.
10
.
Let
\gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the unique closed curve formed by the line-segments between endpoints of edges in
*\partial^{\mathrm{edge}}_{\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}}\mathcal{C}\subset\mathbb{E}^{\circ}
such that
\mathcal{C}
is contained in the bounded connected component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\gamma
.
Denote the closure of this component by
\mathcal{P}
, and define the
interface

\Gamma=\Gamma_{\mathtt{ATRC}}^{n,m}
by

\Gamma:=\mathcal{P}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}\subseteq\bar{V}.

Figure 10.
Left: A realisation of
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{v_{L}}
(endpoints of solid edges) in
K
for
n=m=3
, the dual
*\partial^{\mathrm{edge}}_{\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}}\mathcal{C}
of its edge-boundary in
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
(dashed edges), and the surrounding polygon
\mathcal{P}
(grey). Right: a path in
*\partial^{\mathrm{edge}}_{K}\mathcal{C}
connecting
(-n-\tfrac{3}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})
and
(n+\tfrac{3}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})
on which
\sigma_{\circ}=+1
(red dashed edges), and a path in
*\partial^{\mathrm{edge}}_{K}\mathcal{C}
connecting
(-n-\tfrac{1}{2},-\tfrac{1}{2})
and
(n+\tfrac{1}{2},-\tfrac{1}{2})
on which
\sigma_{\circ}=-1
(green dashed edges).

5.
Weak mixing in the ATRC

The main results proved in this section are the single-edge relaxation of the ATRC, Proposition
3.2
, and the
ratio
weak mixing property for the ATRC, Theorem
3.3
.

In Subsections
5.1
and
5.2
we define the height function of the six-vertex model and use
[
GP23
]
to show that it relaxes exponentially to its infinite-volume limit.
In Subsection
5.3
, we show that this implies exponential relaxation for the ATRC measure with “
0,1
” boundary conditions, stated in Proposition
5.12
.
In Subsection
5.4
, we derive Proposition
3.2
from Proposition
5.12
and an input from
[
ADG24
]
, stated in Proposition
5.14
.
In Subsection
5.5
, we show how to derive from Proposition
3.2
the
exponential
weak mixing property of the ATRC, stated in Theorem
5.15
.
Finally, we prove Theorem
3.3
using the classical work of Alexander
[
Ale98
]
, Theorem
5.15
and the imput, Proposition
5.14
, from
[
ADG24
]
.

5.1.
The six-vertex height function

The proof of Proposition
3.2
relies on exponential relaxation of the ATRC measures with “
0,1
” boundary conditions. The latter will be established via the height function representation of the six-vertex model and the BKW coupling
[
BKW76
]
with FK-percolation, while it also requires some input from
[
GP23
]
.
We first introduce the six-vertex height function and provide the necessary graph definitions.

5.1.1.
Six-vertex height function

Recall the edge orientations and spin representation of the six-vertex model introduced in Section
4.1
.
A six-vertex
height function
is an assignment of integers, called
heights
, to the vertices in both
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbb{L}_{\circ}
.
Given edge orientations that satisfy the ice rule, define
h
on
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}\cup\mathbb{L}_{\circ}
as follows. Fix an integer height at some arbitrary fixed vertex. Then iteratively define the heights at other vertices by increasing the height by 1 when traversing an edge
e\in\mathbb{E}^{\diamond}
from its left to its right (with respect to its assigned orientation) and decreasing the height by 1 when traversing an arrow from its right to its left; see Fig.
8
.
As with the spins, the procedure is self-consistent due to the ice rule.
Note that the heights on
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
and on
\mathbb{L}_{\circ}
automatically have different parity.
By convention, we set the parity on
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
to be even.
The gradient of
h
is in bijective correspondence with the edge orientations and hence with the spin representation, up to a global spin flip, as demonstrated by the following relation (see Fig.
8
):

h(u)-h(i)=\sigma_{\bullet}(i)\sigma_{\circ}(u)\qquad\text{for any }t\in\mathbb{L}_{\diamond}\text{ with }i\in e_{t},\,u\in e_{t}^{*}.

On the other hand, up to a global spin flip, the spins are obtained from the height function by setting, for all
i\in\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
and
u\in\mathbb{L}_{\circ}
,

(19)

\sigma_{\bullet}(i)=\begin{cases}+1&\text{ if }h(i)=0\mod{4}\\
-1&\text{ if }h(i)=2\mod{4}\end{cases},\quad\sigma_{\circ}(u)=\begin{cases}+1&\text{ if }h(u)=1\mod{4}\\
-1&\text{ if }h(u)=3\mod{4}\end{cases}.

This motivates the following definition.

Definition 5.1
.

A function
{h:\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}\cup\mathbb{L}_{\circ}\to\mathbb{Z}}
is called a (six-vertex) height function if it satisfies the following:

•

for any
t\in\mathbb{L}_{\diamond}
,
i\in t\cap\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
, and
u\in t\cap\mathbb{L}_{\circ}
, one has
|h(i)-h(u)|=1
,

•

for any
i\in\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
, one has
h(i)\in 2\mathbb{Z}
.

Denote the set of all height functions by
\Omega_{\mathrm{hf}}
.
The type of a tile
t\in\mathbb{L}_{\diamond}
in a height function
h
is given by the type of its gradient function; see Fig.
8
.
Fix
g\in\Omega_{\mathrm{hf}}
, parameters
\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}>0
and a finite subset
\Delta\subset\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}\cup\mathbb{L}_{\circ}
.
The set
A:=A_{\Delta}\subset\mathbb{L}_{\diamond}
of tiles of
\Delta
is given by the tiles with at least one corner in
\Delta
. The set
\partial A\subseteq A
of boundary tiles of
\Delta
is given by the tiles with precisely one corner in
\Delta
.
Let
\delta\subseteq\partial A
.
The corresponding height function measure on
\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}\cup\mathbb{L}_{\circ}}
is defined by

(20)

\mathtt{HF}_{\Delta,\delta;\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}}^{g}(h)\propto\mathbf{c}^{|T_{5,6}^{A\setminus\delta}(h)|}\,\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}^{|T_{5,6}^{\delta}(h)|}\,\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{\mathrm{hf}}}(h)\,\mathds{1}_{h(x)=g(x)\,\forall x\in(\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}\cup\mathbb{L}_{\circ})\setminus\Delta}\,,

where
T_{5,6}^{A\setminus\delta}(h)
(resp.
T_{5,6}^{\delta}(h)
) is the set of tiles
t\in A\setminus\delta
(resp.
t\in\delta
) of type 5-6 in
h
.

If
g
is constant
n
on
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
and constant
m
on
\mathbb{L}_{\circ}
(
n
even and
m=n\pm 1
), we simply write
\mathtt{HF}_{\Delta,\delta;\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}}^{n,m}
.
When
\delta=\partial A
, we omit
\delta
from the subscript. When
\delta=\varnothing
, we omit
\delta
and
\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}
from the subscript.

Figure 11.
Left: part of the rotated square lattice
\mathbb{L}
. Its faces are the tiles in
\mathbb{L}_{\diamond}
. Center: part of the augmented lattice
\overline{\mathbb{L}}
. Right:
\mathbb{L}
-domains given by the vertices strictly within simple circuits in
\overline{\mathbb{L}},\,\mathbb{L}_{\bullet},\,\mathbb{L}_{\circ}
, respectively. The lower left and right
\mathbb{L}
-domains are even and odd, respectively.

5.1.2.
Rotated lattice and domains

Consider the rotated square lattice
\mathbb{L}
with vertex-set
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}\cup\mathbb{L}_{\circ}
and edges between nearest neighbours, that is, between vertices of Euclidean distance
1/\sqrt{2}
.
Given
\Delta\subseteq\mathbb{L}
, we write
\Delta_{\bullet}=\Delta\cap\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
and
\Delta_{\circ}=\Delta\cap\mathbb{L}_{\circ}
.
The augmented graph
\overline{\mathbb{L}}
has the same vertex-set as
\mathbb{L}
and all edges of
\mathbb{L},\,\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbb{L}_{\circ}
, see Fig.
11
.
We restrict the notion of simple circuits in
\overline{\mathbb{L}}
to those that do not traverse both
e
and
e^{*}
for any
e\in\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}
, so that they can be embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
We identify such circuits with their planar embedding.

Definition 5.2

(
\mathbb{L}
-domains)
.

A finite subset
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathbb{L}
is called an
\mathbb{L}
-domain if there exists a simple circuit
C
in
\overline{\mathbb{L}}
such that
\mathcal{D}
is given by the vertices of
\mathbb{L}
strictly within
C
, that is, in the bounded connected component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus C
.
It is called
even
(respectively,
odd
) if
\partial^{\mathrm{ex}}_{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{D}\subset\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
(respectively,
\partial^{\mathrm{ex}}_{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{D}\subset\mathbb{L}_{\circ}
); see Fig.
11
.

Definition 5.3

(
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
- and
\mathbb{L}_{\circ}
-domains)
.

A subgraph
G=(V,E)
of
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
is called an
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
-domain of the
first kind
if
E=\mathbb{E}_{V}
and
V
is given by the vertices within (see Definition
4.8
) a simple circuit in
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
.
We define
\mathbb{L}_{\circ}
-domains of the first kind in the same manner.
We say that a subgraph
G=(V,E)
of
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
is a domain of the
second kind
if its dual
(\mathbb{V}_{*E},*E)
is an
\mathbb{L}_{\circ}
-domain of the first kind.
It is called an
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
-domain if it is an
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
-domain of the first or second kind.
We define
\mathbb{L}_{\circ}
-domains (of the second kind) analogously; see Fig.
12
.

Given a subgraph
G=(V,E)
of
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
with dual
G^{*}=(\mathbb{V}_{*E},*E)
, define
\Delta_{G}
as the set of vertices of degree
4
in
G
and
G^{*}
, that is,

\Delta_{G}:=\{i\in V:|\partial^{\mathrm{edge}}_{G}\{i\}|=4\}\cup\{u\in\mathbb{V}_{*E}:|\partial^{\mathrm{edge}}_{G^{*}}\{u\}|=4\}.

Observe that, if
G
is an
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
-domain, then
\mathcal{D}_{G}:=\Delta_{G}
is an
\mathbb{L}
-domain. In this case, any tile
t\in\mathbb{L}_{\diamond}
intersects
\mathcal{D}_{G}
precisely if its associated edge
e_{t}\in\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}
belongs to
E
, that is,
\{e_{t}:t\in A_{\mathcal{D}_{G}}\}=E
; see Section
5.1.1
and Fig.
12
.

Figure 12.
Left: a circuit in
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
(thick black edges) and the corresponding
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
-domain of the first kind (thin and thick black edges).
Right: a circuit in
\mathbb{L}_{\circ}
(dashed edges) obtained by shifting the left one by
(1/2,1/2)
and the corresponding
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
-domain of the second kind (black edges).
The tiles corresponding to the edges of the domains are shaded grey, and the vertices of the corresponding
\mathbb{L}
-domains are surrounded by red circles.

5.1.3.
Duality coupling with the AT model

The ATRC measures can be sampled locally from the HF measures, which is the content of the following lemma.
We build on
[
GP23
]
, where a marginal of the ATRC measure was sampled.
Given
\mathbf{c}>2
, a height function
h\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{L}}
, an edge
e=ij\in\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}
with
e^{*}=uv
, and
x\in[0,1]
, define
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{c}}(h,e,x)\in\{(0,0),(0,1),(1,1)\}
as follows:

(21)

\displaystyle\bullet\text{ if }h(u)\neq h(v),\text{ set }\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{c}}(h,e,x)=(1,1),

\displaystyle\bullet\text{ if }h(i)\neq h(j),\text{ set }\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{c}}(h,e,x)=(0,0),

\displaystyle\bullet\text{ if }h(u)=h(v)\text{ and }h(i)=h(j),\text{ set }

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{c}}(h,e,x)=\mathds{1}_{[0,\frac{1}{\mathbf{c}})}(x)\cdot(1,1)+\mathds{1}_{[\frac{1}{\mathbf{c}},\frac{2}{\mathbf{c}})}(x)\cdot(0,0)+\mathds{1}_{[\frac{2}{\mathbf{c}},1]}(x)\cdot(0,1).

Observe that, in order to define
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{c}}(h,e,x)
, it suffices to know the values of the corresponding six-vertex spins
\sigma_{\bullet}(i),\sigma_{\bullet}(j),\sigma_{\circ}(u),\sigma_{\circ}(v)
, defined in (
19
).

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
0<J<U
satisfy
\sinh 2J=e^{-2U}
and take
\mathbf{c}:=\coth 2J
.
Let
G=(V,E)
be an
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
-domain, and let
\mathcal{D}:=\mathcal{D}_{G}
be its associated
\mathbb{L}
-domain.
Let
h
be distributed according to
\mathtt{HF}_{\mathcal{D};\mathbf{c}}^{0,1}
, and let
(U_{e})_{e\in E}
be a sequence of i.i.d. uniform random variables on
[0,1]
, independent of
h
.
Define
\omega_{\tau},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}}
by setting, for
e\in\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}
,

(22)

(\omega_{\tau}(e),\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}(e)):=\begin{cases}(0,1)&\text{if }e\in\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}\setminus E,\\
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{c}}(h,e,U_{e})&\text{if }e\in E.\end{cases}

Then, the law of
(\omega_{\tau},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})
is given by
\mathtt{ATRC}_{G;J,U}^{0,1}
.

Proof.

Denote the underlying probability measure by
\mathbf{P}
.
Recall that a tile
t\in\mathbb{L}_{\diamond}
intersects
\mathcal{D}
precisely if its associated edge
e_{t}\in\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}
belongs to
E
, see Fig.
12
.
Let
(\sigma_{\bullet},\sigma_{\circ})
with values in
\{\pm 1\}^{\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}}\times\{\pm 1\}^{\mathbb{L}_{\circ}}
be the spin configurations obtained from
h
by (
19
).
For ease of notation, we use the same symbols for the random variables and their realisations.
For
\sigma_{\bullet}\in\{\pm 1\}^{\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}},\,\sigma_{\circ}\in\{\pm 1\}^{\mathbb{L}_{\circ}},\,\omega\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}}
and
\#\in\{0,1\}
, define

\Sigma_{\mathcal{D}_{\bullet}}^{+}(\sigma_{\bullet}):=\mathds{1}_{\sigma_{\bullet}\equiv 1\text{ on }\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{\bullet}},\quad\Sigma_{\mathcal{D}_{\circ}}^{+}(\sigma_{\circ}):=\mathds{1}_{\sigma_{\circ}\equiv 1\text{ on }\mathbb{L}_{\circ}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{\circ}},\quad\Omega_{E}^{\#}(\omega)=\mathds{1}_{\omega\equiv\#\text{ on }\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}\setminus E}.

Then, the law of the quadruple
(\sigma_{\bullet},\sigma_{\circ},\omega_{\tau},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})
can be written as follows:

\mathbf{P}(\sigma_{\bullet},\sigma_{\circ},\omega_{\tau},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})\propto\mathds{1}_{\mathrm{ice}}(\sigma_{\bullet},\sigma_{\circ})\,\Sigma_{\mathcal{D}_{\bullet}}^{+}(\sigma_{\bullet})\,\Sigma_{\mathcal{D}_{\circ}}^{+}(\sigma_{\circ})\,\mathds{1}_{\sigma_{\bullet}\sim\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}}\,\mathds{1}_{\sigma_{\circ}\sim\omega_{\tau}^{*}}\\
\cdot(\mathbf{c}-2)^{\absolutevalue{(\omega_{\tau\tau'}\setminus\omega_\tau)\cap E}}\,\mathds{1}_{\omega_{\tau}\subseteq\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}}\,\Omega_{E}^{0}(\omega_{\tau})\,\Omega_{E}^{1}(\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}).

Observe that
\sigma_{\bullet}\sim\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}},\,\sigma_{\circ}\sim\omega_{\tau}^{*}
and
\omega_{\tau}\subseteq\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}
imply that
(\sigma_{\bullet},\sigma_{\circ})
satisfies the ice rule.
Summing over
\sigma_{\bullet}
and
\sigma_{\circ}
, we obtain the law of
(\omega_{\tau},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})
:

\mathbf{P}(\omega_{\tau},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})\propto 2^{\kappa_{V}(\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})+\kappa_{V^{\prime}}(\omega_{\tau}^{*})}\,\Omega_{E}^{0}(\omega_{\tau})\,\Omega_{E}^{1}(\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})\,\mathds{1}_{\omega_{\tau}\subseteq\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}}\,(\mathbf{c}-2)^{\absolutevalue{(\omega_{\tau\tau'}\setminus\omega_\tau)\cap E}},

where
V^{\prime}:=\mathbb{V}_{*E}
, and where we used that there exist
2^{\kappa_{V}(\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})-1}
spin configurations
\sigma_{\bullet}\sim\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}
with
\Sigma_{\mathcal{D}_{\bullet}}^{+}(\sigma_{\bullet})=1
, and similarly for
\sigma_{\circ}\sim\omega_{\tau}^{*}
.
By planar duality,
\kappa_{V^{\prime}}(\omega_{\tau}^{*})=\kappa_{V}(\omega_{\tau})+\absolutevalue{\omega_\tau\cap E}+\mathrm{const}(G)
.
Since
\mathrm{w}_{\tau},\mathrm{w}_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}
from (
7
) satisfy
\mathrm{w}_{\tau}=2
and
\mathrm{w}_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}=\mathbf{c}-2
, the proof is complete.
∎

5.1.4.
Baxter–Kelland–Wu coupling

In Section
4.2
, we described the BKW coupling by sampling the FK-percolation from the six-vertex model.
We now focus on the reverse direction of the BKW.
Fix
q>4
and
\beta=\beta_{c}(q)=\ln(1+\sqrt{q})
, and let
\lambda,\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}
be as in (
4
) and (
5
).
Recall the loop representation of FK-percolation and the representations of the six-vertex model introduced in Section
4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be an even
\mathbb{L}
-domain, and set

(23)

E:=*\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}_{\circ}}\subset\mathbb{E}^{\bullet},\quad\Lambda:=\mathbb{V}_{E}\subset\mathbb{L}_{\bullet},\quad\text{and}\quad G:=(\Lambda,E).

The following result
[
BKW76
]
is classical. It may be proved through the arguments presented in the proof of Lemma
4.2
; see
[
GP23
, Theorem 7]
for a proof in a similar setting.
We will construct a height function from a loop configuration by increasing or decreasing the height whenever we cross a loop, which is the same as orienting the loops as in Section
4.2
. We chose the first option for the sake of brevity.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
\omega
be a random element of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}}
distributed according to
\mathtt{FK}_{G;\beta,q}^{\mathrm{w}}
. Consider the corresponding loop configuration
\mathrm{loop}(\omega)
, and define a height function
h
as follows:

H1

Set
h(i)=0
for
i\in\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}\setminus\mathcal{D}
, and
h(u)=1
for
u\in\mathbb{L}_{\circ}\setminus\mathcal{D}
.

H2

Assign constant values to clusters of
\omega
and
\omega^{*}
in
\mathcal{D}
by
decreasing
the height by 1 with probability
e^{\lambda}/\sqrt{q}
and
increasing
the height by 1 with probability
e^{-\lambda}/\sqrt{q}
when crossing a loop from outside, independently for every loop.

The height function
h
is distributed according to
\mathtt{HF}_{\mathcal{D};\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}}^{0,1}
.

The above procedure also works for odd
\mathbb{L}
-domains with the difference that one has to take
\omega\sim\mathtt{FK}_{\mathcal{D}_{\bullet};p,q}^{\mathrm{f}}
, and
H2
must be replaced by

H2’

Assign constant values to clusters of
\omega
and
\omega^{*}
in
\mathcal{D}
by
decreasing
the height by 1 with probability
e^{\lambda}/\sqrt{q}
and
increasing
the height by 1 with probability
e^{-\lambda}/\sqrt{q}
when crossing a loop from outside, independently for every loop.

5.1.5.
Input from
[
GP23
]

For the whole section, fix
q>4
and
\beta=\beta_{c}(q)=\ln(1+\sqrt{q})
, and let
\lambda,\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}
be as in (
4
) and (
5
).
The following proposition is a consequence of
[
GP23
, Proposition 6.1 and Lemma 6.2]
and their proofs.

Proposition 5.6
.

For any sequence of even or odd
\mathbb{L}
-domains
\mathcal{D}_{k}\nearrow\mathbb{L}
, the measures
\mathtt{HF}_{\mathcal{D}_{k};\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}}^{0,1}
converge to some
\mathtt{HF}_{\mathbf{c}}^{0,1}
which is independent of the sequence
(\mathcal{D}_{k})
. Moreover, the limiting measure
\mathtt{HF}_{\mathbf{c}}^{0,1}
can be constructed in either of the following two ways:

(i)

Sample
\omega\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}}
according to
\mathtt{FK}_{\beta,q}^{\mathrm{w}}
. Set
h=0
on the unique infinite cluster of
\omega
, and sample
h
elsewhere according to
H2
in Section
5.1.4
.

(ii)

Sample
\omega\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}}
according to
\mathtt{FK}_{\beta,q}^{\mathrm{f}}
. Set
h=1
on the unique infinite cluster of
\omega^{*}
, and sample
h
elsewhere according to
H2’
in Section
5.1.4
.

The following lemma is a slight generalisation of
[
GP23
, Eq. (28)]
and can be proved in exactly the same manner.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be an
\mathbb{L}
-domain, and let
\delta
be a set of boundary tiles of
\mathcal{D}
. Define
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{even}}=\mathcal{D}\setminus(\partial^{\mathrm{in}}\mathcal{D})_{\bullet}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{odd}}=\mathcal{D}\setminus(\partial^{\mathrm{in}}\mathcal{D})_{\circ}
, where the boundaries are taken in
\mathbb{L}
. Then,
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{even}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{odd}}
are disjoint unions of even and odd domains in
\mathbb{L}
, respectively. Moreover, the following stochastic ordering of measures holds:

\mathtt{HF}_{\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{even}};\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}}^{0,1}\leq_{\mathrm{st}}\mathtt{HF}_{\mathcal{D},\delta;\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}}^{0,1}\leq_{\mathrm{st}}\mathtt{HF}_{\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{odd}};\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}}^{0,1}.

This lemma readily implies that
\mathtt{HF}_{\mathcal{D}_{k},\delta_{k};\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}}^{0,1}
(in particular
\mathtt{HF}_{\mathcal{D}_{k};\mathbf{c}}^{0,1}
) converges to the same limit, no matter which sequences of
\mathcal{D}_{k}
and
\delta_{k}
we chose.

5.2.
Relaxation of height function measures

The aim of this section is to prove a relaxation statement for the six-vertex height function measures with modified weight
\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}
on arbitrary boundary tiles, in particular for the measure with unmodified weight
\mathbf{c}
on all tiles.
For the whole section, fix
q>4
and
\beta=\beta_{c}(q)=\ln(1+\sqrt{q})
, and let
\lambda,\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}
be as in (
4
) and (
5
).

Given a measure
\mathtt{HF}
on
\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{L}}
and
\Delta\subset\mathbb{L}
, define
\mathtt{HF}|_{\Delta}
as its marginal on
\mathbb{Z}^{\Delta}
.

Proposition 5.8
.

There exist constants
c,\alpha>0
such that, for every finite
\Delta\subset\mathbb{L}
, every
\mathbb{L}
-domain
\mathcal{D}
that contains
\Delta
, and every set
\delta\subset\mathbb{L}_{\diamond}
of boundary tiles of
\mathcal{D}
,

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}\left(\mathtt{HF}_{\mathcal{D},\delta;\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}}^{0,1}|_{\Delta},\mathtt{HF}_{\mathbf{c}}^{0,1}|_{\Delta})\right)<c\,\absolutevalue{\Delta}\,(\mathrm{diam}(\Delta)+\mathrm{d}_{\infty}(\Delta,\mathcal{D}^{c}))^{2}\,e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{d}_{\infty}(\Delta,\mathcal{D}^{c})}.

The statement on even or odd
\mathbb{L}
-domains and with modified weight
\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}
on all boundary tiles can be proven in the same way as
[
ADG24
, Proposition 4.2]
, which is slightly less general. We provide only the statement.

Lemma 5.9
.

There exist constants
c,\alpha>0
such that, for every finite
\Delta\subset\mathbb{L}
and every even or odd
\mathbb{L}
-domain
\mathcal{D}
that contains
\Delta
,

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}\left(\mathtt{HF}_{\mathcal{D};\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}}^{0,1}|_{\Delta},\mathtt{HF}_{\mathbf{c}}^{0,1}|_{\Delta}\right)<c\,(\mathrm{diam}(\Delta)+\mathrm{d}_{\infty}(\Delta,\mathcal{D}^{c}))\,e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{d}_{\infty}(\Delta,\mathcal{D}^{c})}.

Before deducing Proposition
5.8
from the above and the stochastic ordering of height function measures, Lemma
5.7
, we need a general lemma.

Lemma 5.10
.

[
HS22
, Lemma 2.16]

Let
\mu
and
\nu
be two probability measures on a finite
totally ordered
set
S
with
\mu\leq_{\mathrm{st}}\nu
. Let
\mathbf{P}
be a monotone coupling of
X\sim\mu
and
Y\sim\nu
. Then,

\mathbf{P}(X\neq Y)\leq(\absolutevalue{S}-1)\,\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mu,\nu).

Proof.

Identify
S
with
\{0,\dots,\absolutevalue{S}-1\}
.
Let
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{op}}
be an optimal coupling of
X
and
Y
, that is,
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{op}}(X\neq Y)=\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mu,\nu)
. Since
\mathbf{P}(X\leq Y)=1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(X\neq Y)=\mathbf{P}(Y-X\geq 1)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}[Y-X]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{\mathrm{op}}[Y-X]\leq(\absolutevalue{S}-1)\,\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{op}}(X\neq Y),

where we applied Markov’s inequality.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.8
.

Let
\Delta\subset\mathbb{L}
be finite, let
\mathcal{D}
be an
\mathbb{L}
-domain, and let
\delta\subset\mathbb{L}_{\diamond}
be a set of boundary tiles of
\mathcal{D}
.
Assume without loss of generality that
\Delta\subseteq\mathcal{D}\setminus\partial^{\mathrm{in}}\mathcal{D}
.
Let
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{even}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{odd}}
be as in Lemma
5.7
, and recall the stochastic domination statement therein. Consider a monotone coupling
\mathbf{P}
of
h^{-}\sim\mathtt{HF}_{\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{even}};\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}}^{0,1},\,h\sim\mathtt{HF}_{\mathcal{D},\delta;\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}}^{0,1}
and
h^{+}\sim\mathtt{HF}_{\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{odd}};\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}}^{0,1}
, that is,

\mathbf{P}(h^{-}\leq h\leq h^{+})=1.

Take any vertex
v\in\Delta
and note that, deterministically, the height functions at
v
take values in
[-m,m]
, where
m:=2(\mathrm{diam}(\Delta)+\mathrm{d}_{\infty}(\Delta,\mathcal{D}^{c}))
.
By Lemma
5.10
, we have

(24)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(h^{-}(v)\neq h(v))

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{P}(h^{-}(v)\neq h^{+}(v))\leq 2m\,\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(\mathtt{HF}_{\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{even}};\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}}^{0,1}|_{\Delta},\mathtt{HF}_{\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{odd}};\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}}^{0,1}|_{\Delta}\big)

\displaystyle\leq 2m\,\big(\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(\mathtt{HF}_{\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{even}};\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}}^{0,1}|_{\Delta},\mathtt{HF}_{\mathbf{c}}^{0,1}|_{\Delta}\big)+\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(\mathtt{HF}_{\mathbf{c}}^{0,1}|_{\Delta},\mathtt{HF}_{\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{odd}};\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}}^{0,1}|_{\Delta}\big)\big).

Now,
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{even}}
is a disjoint union of even
\mathbb{L}
-domains
\mathcal{D}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{D}_{n}
with
\partial^{\mathrm{ex}}\mathcal{D}_{i}\cap\mathcal{D}_{j}=\varnothing
for any
i\neq j
. There exists an
i
with
\Delta\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{i}
and
\mathrm{d}_{\infty}(\Delta,\mathcal{D}_{i}^{c})\geq\mathrm{d}_{\infty}(\Delta,\mathcal{D}^{c})-1
. Moreover, the definition of the height function measures (
20
) readily implies that, for any event
A\subset\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{L}}
depending only on values in
\Delta
, we have
\mathtt{HF}_{\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{even}};\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}}^{0,1}(A)=\mathtt{HF}_{\mathcal{D}_{i};\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}}^{0,1}(A)
. Similar reasoning applies to
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{odd}}
. By Lemma
5.9
, there exist
c,\alpha>0
, such that the right-hand side of (
24
) is bounded by
c\,m^{2}\,e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{d}_{\infty}(\Delta,\mathcal{D}^{c})}
.
The union bound gives

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}\left(\mathtt{HF}_{\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{even}};\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}}^{0,1}|_{\Delta},\mathtt{HF}_{\mathcal{D},\delta;\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{b}}}^{0,1}|_{\Delta}\right)\leq\mathbf{P}(h^{-}|_{\Delta}\neq h|_{\Delta})\leq\sum_{v\in\Delta}\mathbf{P}(h^{-}(v)\neq h(v)).

Another application of the triangle inequality and of Lemma
5.9
finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.11
.

Using that
h^{-},h^{+}
have uniformly bounded second moment (localisation) and adapting Lemma
5.10
allow to remove the square of
(\mathrm{diam}(\Delta)+\mathrm{d}_{\infty}(\Delta,\mathcal{D}^{c}))^{2}
in the bound in Proposition
5.8
.

5.3.
Relaxation of ATRC measures

The proof of Proposition
3.2
is based on Proposition
5.14
and another one below, which is a consequence of Proposition
5.8
.

The following proposition establishes exponential relaxation of the ATRC measures on domains and with “
0,1
” boundary conditions. Once Proposition
3.2
is proven, it can be concluded that the limit coincides with the unique ATRC Gibbs measure. Recall the definition (
21
) of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{c}}
.

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
0<J<U
satisfy
\sinh 2J=e^{-2U}
. There exists a measure
\mathtt{ATRC}_{J,U}
on
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}}\times\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}}
and constants
c,\alpha>0
such that, for any
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
-domain
G=(V,E)
and any
F\subseteq E
,

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}\left(\mathtt{ATRC}_{G;J,U}^{0,1}[(\omega_{\tau}|_{F},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}|_{F})\in\cdot\,],\mathtt{ATRC}_{J,U}[(\omega_{\tau}|_{F},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}|_{F})\in\cdot\,]\right)\\
<c\,\absolutevalue{\bbV_F}\,(\mathrm{diam}(\mathbb{V}_{F})+\mathrm{d}_{\infty}(\mathbb{V}_{F},V^{c}))^{2}\,e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{d}_{\infty}(\mathbb{V}_{F},V^{c})},

where
\mathrm{diam}
denotes the diameter with respect to
\mathrm{d}_{\infty}
.
Furthermore, the measure
\mathtt{ATRC}_{J,U}
is constructed as follows.
Let
\mathbf{c}=\coth 2J
, let
h
be distributed according to
\mathtt{HF}_{\mathbf{c}}^{0,1}
, and let
(U_{e})_{e\in\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}}
be a sequence of i.i.d. uniform random variables on
[0,1]
, independent of
h
.
The measure
\mathtt{ATRC}_{J,U}
is given by the law of
(\omega_{\tau},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}}\times\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}}
defined by

(\omega_{\tau}(e),\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}(e)):=\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{c}}(h,e,U_{e}),\ e\in\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}.

Remark 5.13
.

It is possible to improve the bound in Proposition
5.12
and remove the square in
(\mathrm{diam}(\mathbb{V}_{F})+\mathrm{d}_{\infty}(\mathbb{V}_{F},V^{c}))^{2}
, see Remark
5.11
.

The above proposition follows from the analogous statement for the height function measures, Proposition
5.8
, and the fact that the ATRC measures can be sampled locally from these measures, which is the content of Lemma
5.4
.

Proof of Proposition
5.12
.

Let
0<J<U
satisfy
\sinh 2J=e^{-2U}
and
\mathbf{c}=\coth 2J
.
Let
G=(V,E)
be an
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
-domain and
\mathcal{D}:=\mathcal{D}_{G}
be its associated
\mathbb{L}
-domain.
Recall that a tile
t\in\mathbb{L}_{\diamond}
intersects
\mathcal{D}
precisely if its associated primal edge
e_{t}\in\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}
belongs to
E
, see Fig.
12
.
Let
F\subset E
, and set
\Delta:=\mathbb{V}_{F}\cup\mathbb{V}_{*F}
.

Take
h\sim\mathtt{HF}_{\mathcal{D};\mathbf{c}}^{0,1}
and
h^{\prime}\sim\mathtt{HF}_{\mathbf{c}}^{0,1}
, and consider an optimal coupling of
h|_{\Delta}
and
h^{\prime}|_{\Delta}
. Let
(U_{e})_{e\in\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}}
be a sequence of i.i.d. uniform random variables on
[0,1]
, independent of
(h,h^{\prime})
. Denote the corresponding probability measure by
\mathbf{P}
.
Define
\omega_{\tau},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}}
from
h
and
(U_{e})
as in (
22
). Define
\omega^{\prime}_{\tau},\omega^{\prime}_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}
from
h^{\prime}
and
(U_{e})
by setting
(\omega^{\prime}_{\tau}(e),\omega^{\prime}_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}(e))=\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{c}}(h^{\prime},e,U_{e})
for
e\in\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}
.
Then, clearly

(\omega_{\tau}|_{F},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}|_{F})\neq(\omega^{\prime}_{\tau}|_{F},\omega^{\prime}_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}|_{F})\quad\text{implies}\quad h|_{\Delta}\neq h^{\prime}|_{\Delta}.

Therefore, by the definition of an optimal coupling and by Proposition
5.8
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\big((\omega_{\tau}|_{F},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}|_{F})\neq(\omega^{\prime}_{\tau}|_{F},\omega^{\prime}_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}|_{F})\big)

\displaystyle<c\,\absolutevalue{\Delta}\,(\mathrm{diam}(\Delta)+\mathrm{d}_{\infty}(\Delta,\mathcal{D}^{c}))^{2}\,e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{d}_{\infty}(\Delta,\mathcal{D}^{c})}

\displaystyle<c^{\prime}\,\absolutevalue{F}\,(\mathrm{diam}(\mathbb{V}_{F})+\mathrm{d}_{\infty}(\mathbb{V}_{F},V^{c}))^{2}\,e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{d}_{\infty}(\mathbb{V}_{F},V^{c})},

for some
c,c^{\prime},\alpha>0
.
By Lemma
5.4
, the law of
(\omega_{\tau},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})
is given by
\mathtt{ATRC}_{G;J,U}^{0,1}
. Finally, denote the law of
(\omega^{\prime}_{\tau},\omega^{\prime}_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})
by
\mathtt{ATRC}_{J,U}
, and the proof is complete.
∎

5.4.
Proof of Proposition
3.2

Finally, we derive Proposition
3.2
from Proposition
5.12
and the following one that we import from
[
ADG24
]
.

Proposition 5.14
.

[
ADG24
, Proposition 1.1]

Let
0<J<U
satisfy
\sinh 2J=e^{-2U}
. There exists
c>0
such that, for every
n\geq 1
,

\mathtt{ATRC}_{\Lambda_{n};J,U}^{1,1}(0\xleftrightarrow{\omega_{\tau}}\partial^{\mathrm{ex}}\Lambda_{n})<e^{-cn}.

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

Let
\sigma\in\{\tau,\tau\tau^{\prime}\}
. Assume without loss of generality that
n=2k
is even (the other case is treated similarly). We proceed in two steps.

Step 1.
There exists
\alpha>0
such that

\mathtt{ATRC}_{\Lambda_{n};J,U}^{1,1}[\omega_{\sigma}(e)]-\mathtt{ATRC}_{\Lambda_{n};J,U}^{0,1}[\omega_{\sigma}(e)]\leq e^{-\alpha n}.

Let
(\omega_{\tau},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})
be distributed according to
\mathtt{ATRC}_{\Lambda_{n};J,U}^{1,1}
.
Define
\mathcal{C}
to be the outermost (dual) circuit in
\omega_{\tau}^{*}
that surrounds
\Lambda_{k}
and is contained in
\Lambda_{2k}
if it exists, otherwise set
\mathcal{C}=\varnothing
.
By exponential decay of connection probabilities in
\omega_{\tau}
, Proposition
5.14
, there exist
c,\alpha>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathtt{ATRC}_{\Lambda_{n};J,U}^{1,1}[\omega_{\sigma}(e)]

\displaystyle\leq\mathtt{ATRC}_{\Lambda_{n};J,U}^{1,1}\left[\omega_{\sigma}(e)\,\big|\,\mathcal{C}\neq\varnothing\right]+\mathtt{ATRC}_{\Lambda_{n};J,U}^{1,1}[\Lambda_{k}\xleftrightarrow{\omega_{\tau}}\partial^{\mathrm{in}}\Lambda_{2k}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathtt{ATRC}_{\Lambda_{n};J,U}^{1,1}\left[\omega_{\sigma}(e)\,\big|\,\mathcal{C}\neq\varnothing\right]+cke^{-\alpha k},

where we also used the strong FKG and DLR properties of the ATRC measures (Lemma
3.1
and (
DLR
) in Section
3
).
Now, again by the strong FKG and (
DLR
) and by exponential relaxation, Proposition
5.12
, there exist
c^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathtt{ATRC}_{\Lambda_{n};J,U}^{1,1}

\displaystyle\left[\omega_{\sigma}(e)\,\big|\,\mathcal{C}\neq\varnothing\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{C}\mathtt{ATRC}_{\Lambda_{n};J,U}^{1,1}\left[\omega_{\sigma}(e)\,\big|\,\mathcal{C}=C\right]\mathtt{ATRC}_{\Lambda_{n};J,U}^{1,1}\left[\mathcal{C}=C\,\big|\,\mathcal{C}\neq\varnothing\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{C}\mathtt{ATRC}_{G_{C};J,U}^{0,1}[\omega_{\sigma}(e)]\ \mathtt{ATRC}_{\Lambda_{n};J,U}^{1,1}\left[\mathcal{C}=C\,\big|\,\mathcal{C}\neq\varnothing\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathtt{ATRC}_{\Lambda_{n};J,U}^{0,1}\left[\omega_{\sigma}(e)\right]+c^{\prime}k^{2}e^{-\alpha^{\prime}k},

where the summation is over all realisations
C
of
\mathcal{C}
, and where
G_{C}
is the largest
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
-domain of the first kind within
C
that contains
\Lambda_{k}
. This proves Step 1.

Step 2.
There exists
\alpha>0
such that

\mathtt{ATRC}_{\Lambda_{n};J,U}^{0,1}[\omega_{\sigma}(e)]-\mathtt{ATRC}_{\Lambda_{n};J,U}^{0,0}[\omega_{\sigma}(e)]\leq e^{-\alpha n}.

We use the same strategy as in Step 1. Indeed, let
(\tilde{\omega}_{\tau},\tilde{\omega}_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})
be distributed according to
\mathtt{ATRC}_{\Lambda_{n};J,U}^{0,0}
. Since, by self-duality (
9
),
\tilde{\omega}_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}^{*}
has the same law as
\omega_{\tau}
(but on the dual graph of
\Lambda_{n}
) from Step 1, Proposition
5.14
allows to find a circuit in
\tilde{\omega}_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}
that surrounds
\Lambda_{k}
and is contained in
\Lambda_{2k}
. For a realisation
C
of an outermost such circuit, consider the largest
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
-domain of the second kind within
C
that contains
\Lambda_{k}
. The remainder of the argument is analogous to Step 1.
∎

5.5.
Ratio weak mixing: proof of Theorem
3.3

We first derive the
exponential
weak mixing property from the single edge relaxation, Proposition
3.2
.
Given
F\subset\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}
and a measure
\mathtt{ATRC}
on
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}}\times\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}}
, we write
\mathtt{ATRC}|_{F}
for its marginal on
\{0,1\}^{F}\times\{0,1\}^{F}
.

Theorem 5.15
.

Let
0<J<U
satisfy
\sinh 2J=e^{-2U}
. There exists
c>0
such that, for any finite subgraph
G=(V,E)
of
\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
and any
F\subset E
,

\sup_{\eta_{\tau},\eta_{\tau\tau^{\prime}},\eta_{\tau}^{\prime},\eta_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}\Big(\mathtt{ATRC}_{G;J,U}^{\eta_{\tau},\eta_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}}|_{F},\mathtt{ATRC}_{G;J,U}^{\eta_{\tau}^{\prime},\eta_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}^{\prime}}|_{F}\Big)\leq 2\sum_{e\in F}e^{-c\,\mathrm{d}_{\infty}(e,V^{c})},

where
\mathrm{d}_{\infty}
is the distance induced by the
L^{\infty}
norm.

As a consequence, the measures
\mathtt{ATRC}_{G;J,U}^{\eta_{\tau},\eta_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}}
converge (as
G\nearrow\mathbb{L}_{\bullet}
) to a limit measure
\mathtt{ATRC}_{J,U}
, which is independent of the choice of boundary conditions
\eta_{\tau},\eta_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}
. Furthermore, the limit
\mathtt{ATRC}_{J,U}
is the unique ATRC
Gibbs
measure.

Proof.

By the strong FKG property, Lemma
3.1
, the supremum is attained for
\eta_{\tau}=\eta_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}=1
and
\eta_{\tau}^{\prime}=\eta_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}^{\prime}=0
. Let
\Psi
be a monotone coupling of
\mathtt{ATRC}_{G;J,U}^{1,1}
and
\mathtt{ATRC}_{G;J,U}^{0,0}
, and let
(X,Y)=\big((X_{\tau},X_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}),(Y_{\tau},Y_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})\big)\sim\Psi
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}\Big(\mathtt{ATRC}_{G;J,U}^{1,1}|_{F},\mathtt{ATRC}_{G;J,U}^{0,0}|_{F}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\Psi(X|_{F}\neq Y|_{F})

\displaystyle\leq\Psi(X_{\tau}|_{F}\neq Y_{\tau}|_{F})+\Psi(X_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}|_{F}\neq Y_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}|_{F}).

Now, for
\sigma\in\{\tau,\tau\tau^{\prime}\}
,

\displaystyle\Psi(X_{\sigma}|_{F}\neq Y_{\sigma}|_{F})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{e\in F}\Psi(X_{\sigma}(e)\neq Y_{\sigma}(e))=\sum_{e\in F}\Psi(X_{\sigma}(e)>Y_{\sigma}(e))

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in F}\left(\Psi(X_{\sigma}(e)=1)-\Psi(Y_{\sigma}(e)=1)\right)\leq\sum_{e\in F}e^{-c\mathrm{d}_{\infty}(e,V^{c})}

for some
c>0
by Proposition
3.2
, where we also used strong positive association and the DLR property of the ATRC measures.
∎

A less trivial consequence is the
ratio
weak mixing property, Theorem
3.3
. This follows from the study performed in
[
Ale98
]
, relying on Theorem
5.15
and Proposition
5.14
.

Proof of Theorem
3.3
.

This is a direct application of
[
Ale98
, Section 5]
. The reasoning there requires two properties of the measure. The first is exponential weak mixing, which is the content of Theorem
5.15
. The second is the admittance of exponentially bounded controlling regions in the sense of
[
Ale98
]
, and we argue its validity as follows.

Let
(\omega_{\tau},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})\sim\mathtt{ATRC}_{G;J,U}^{\eta_{\tau},\eta_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}}
, fix
F\subset E
, and define

H(F)=\{e\in E\setminus F:e\xleftrightarrow{\omega_{\tau}}\mathbb{V}_{F}\text{ or }e^{*}\xleftrightarrow{\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}^{*}}\mathbb{V}_{*F}\}.

Conditionally on the states of the edges in
H(F)
, the values of the field in
F
and
E\setminus(F\cup H(F))
are independent. Moreover, the probability that
e\in E\setminus F
belongs to
H(F)
decays exponentially in
\mathrm{d}_{\infty}(e,\mathbb{V}_{F})
by uniform exponential decay in
\omega_{\tau}
and in the dual of
\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}
. The former is the content of Proposition
5.14
and the latter follows from self-duality.
One can then apply
[
Ale98
, Section 5]
to obtain the desired claim.
∎

6.
Strong mixing

In this section, we use
[
Ott25
]
to push the mixing properties derived in the previous section to strong mixing properties for finite volume ATRC measures.

6.1.
Setup

For
k\geq 1
, let
\Gamma_{k}=((2k+1)\mathbb{Z})^{2}
.
Given
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
or
V\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, denote
[x]_{k}:=x+\{-k,\dots,k\}^{2}
or
[V]_{k}:=\cup_{x\in V}(x+\{-k,\dots,k\}^{2})
respectively.

Path decoupling property.

Let
k\geq 1
. For a simple closed nearest-neighbour path
\gamma
on
\Gamma_{k}
, denote by
F_{\mathrm{in}}(\gamma)
,
F_{\mathrm{out}}(\gamma)
the sets of edges in
\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}
that are, respectively, in the finite connected component of
\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}\setminus\mathbb{E}_{[\gamma]_{k}}
, in the infinite connected component of
\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}\setminus\mathbb{E}_{[\gamma]_{k}}
.

Let
(a,b)\in\{(0,0),(0,1),(1,1)\}^{\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}}
, and
F\subset\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}
finite.
Say that
\mathtt{ATRC}_{F}^{a,b}
has the
k
-decoupling path property
if for any simple closed nearest-neighbour path
\gamma
in
\Gamma_{k}
, and any configuration
(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\in\{(0,0),(0,1),(1,1)\}^{F\cap\mathbb{E}_{[\gamma]_{k}}}
such that

•

the dual of the configuration
\xi_{F}0_{F^{c}}
(
\xi
on
F
and constant
0
on
F^{c}
) contains a simple closed path of open dual edges surrounding
[\mathring{\gamma}]_{k}
, where
\mathring{\gamma}
is the set of sites in
\Gamma_{k}
surrounded by
\gamma
,

•

the configuration
\xi_{F}^{\prime}1_{F^{c}}
(
\xi^{\prime}
on
F
and constant
1
on
F^{c}
) contains a simple closed path of open edges surrounding
[\mathring{\gamma}]_{k}
,

one has that if

(\omega_{\tau},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})\sim\mathtt{ATRC}_{F}^{a,b}\big(\ \,\big|\,\omega_{\tau}(e)=\xi(e),\,\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}(e)=\xi^{\prime}(e)\ \forall e\in\mathbb{E}_{[\gamma]_{k}}\big),

then the restriction of
(\omega_{\tau},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})
to
F\cap F_{\mathrm{in}}(\gamma)
is independent from the restriction of
(\omega_{\tau},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})
to
F\cap F_{\mathrm{out}}(\gamma)
. A configuration
(\xi,\xi^{\prime})
as above is said to contain
a pair of decoupling paths for
\gamma
in
F
.

One has this property in, for example, the case of constant boundary conditions
(0,1)
, or in the case of
F
simply connected and constant
(0,0)
or constant
(1,1)
boundary conditions. More generally, to check if the property holds, it suffices to check that cluster count is factorized by the presence of the required paths in
\mathbb{E}_{[\gamma]_{k}}
, as in Lemma
4.9
.

ATRC as a model on sites.

The setup of
[
Ott25
]
is a collection of site models indexed by finite volumes and some index set
T
. Cast
\mathtt{ATRC}
as a model on sites by taking the spin space at site
x
to be

\Omega_{x}=\{(0,0),(0,1),(1,1)\}^{\{\{x,x+\mathrm{e}_{1}\},\{x,x+\mathrm{e}_{2}\}\}}.

I.e. store the value of the field at a given edge in its left/bottom endpoint. Let

\psi:\{(0,0),(0,1),(1,1)\}^{\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}}\to\bigtimes_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\Omega_{x}

be the natural bijection induced by the above. Let

\mathbb{E}_{BL}(\Lambda)=\big\{\{x,x+\mathrm{e}_{1}\},\{x,x+\mathrm{e}_{2}\}:\ x\in\Lambda\big\}.

Bloc Percolation.

For
p,q\in[0,1]
, let
X_{i},Y_{i}
,
i\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be an independent family of Bernoulli random variables with parameters

P(X_{i}=1)=p,\quad P(Y_{i}=1)=q\quad\forall i\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

For
k\geq 1
,
F\subset\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}
,
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, define
i_{x}\in\Gamma_{k}
to be the unique point with
x\in[i_{x}]_{k}
, and

\eta_{x}=\begin{cases}X_{i_{x}}&\text{ if }\mathbb{E}_{BL}([i_{x}]_{3k+1})\subset F,\\
Y_{i_{x}}&\text{ if }x\in\mathbb{V}_{F},\text{ and }\mathbb{E}_{BL}([i_{x}]_{3k+1})\cap F^{c}\neq\varnothing,\\
0&\text{ else}.\end{cases}

Let
P_{F;k,p,q}
be the law of
(\eta_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
.

6.2.
Strong mixing: main Theorem

Then, the main result of the section is the next Theorem. To get it, we will apply the main theorem of
[
Ott25
]
(see Section
6.3
). For
F_{-},F_{+}\subset F\subset\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}
,
(a,b)\in\{(0,0),(0,1),(1,1)\}^{\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}}
, define

(25)

\nu_{F,F_{-},F_{+}}^{a,b}(\cdot)=\mathtt{ATRC}_{F}^{a,b}\big(\cdot\,|\,\omega_{\tau}(e)=0\ \forall e\in F_{-},\ \omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}(e)=1\ \forall e\in F_{+}\big).

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
0\leq J<U
be such that
\sinh(2J)=e^{-2U}
. There is
l\geq 1
such that: for any
p>0
, there are
L<\infty
and
q<1
such that for any
F\subset\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}
, any
(a,b)\in\{(0,0),(0,1),(1,1)\}^{\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}}
such that
\mathtt{ATRC}_{F}^{a,b}
has the
l
-decoupling path property, any
F_{-},F_{+}\subset\partial^{\mathrm{in}}F
, any
F_{1},F_{2}\subset F
disjoint, and any
(f,g),(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})\in\{(0,0),(0,1),(1,1)\}^{F_{1}}
having positive probability under
\nu_{F,F_{-},F_{+}}^{a,b}
(defined in (
25
))

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(\nu_{F,F_{-},F_{+}}^{a,b}\big(W|_{F_{2}}\in\cdot\,\big|\,W|_{F_{1}}=(f,g)\big),\nu_{F,F_{-},F_{+}}^{a,b}\big(W|_{F_{2}}\in\cdot\,\big|\,W|_{F_{1}}=(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})\big)\big)\\
\leq P_{F\setminus(F_{+}\cup F_{-});L,p,q}\big([\mathbb{V}_{F_{1}}]_{L}\leftrightarrow_{*}[\mathbb{V}_{F_{2}}]_{L}\big),

where
W=(\omega_{\tau},\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}})
, and
\leftrightarrow_{*}
means
*
-connections (connections with
\norm{\ }_{\infty}
nearest-neighbours).

Remark 6.2
.

We will systematically apply this result in cases where
\{x\in\mathbb{V}_{F}:\ \mathbb{E}_{BL}([i_{x}]_{3L+1})\cap F^{c}\neq\varnothing\}
is a very elongated one dimensional object, so exponential decay of the connection probability can be obtained by a coarse-graining argument as in
[
OV18
, Lemma 3.2]
.

6.3.
Verifications of the mixing and decoupling hypotheses

In this section, we apply
[
Ott25
]
to obtain Theorem
6.1
. The setup of
[
Ott25
]
is a family of site models satisfying a collection of hypotheses, denoted Mix1, Mix2, Mar1, Mar2, Mar3. We cast our model in that setup, and check that these hypotheses hold. Theorem
6.1
then follows from
[
Ott25
, Theorem 1.1]

Family of site measures.

Let
l\geq 1
be some number to be fixed large later (it will depend only on
J,U
). In
[
Ott25
]
, a family
(\nu_{\Lambda}^{t})_{\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2},t\in T}
is considered, with
T
some index set. We take
T
to be the set of quintuplets
(F,a,b,F_{-},F_{+})
such that
(a,b)\in\{(0,0),(0,1),(1,1)\}^{\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}}
,
F\subset\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}
finite such that
\mathtt{ATRC}_{F}^{a,b}
has the
l
-decoupling path property, and
F_{-},F_{+}\subset\partial^{\mathrm{in}}F
.

The family
(\nu_{\Lambda}^{t})_{\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2},t\in T}
is then given by

(26)

\nu_{\Lambda}^{a,b,F,F_{+},F_{-}}(\cdot):=\\
\begin{cases}\mathtt{ATRC}_{F}^{a,b}\big(\psi^{-1}(\cdot)\,\big|\,\omega_{\tau}(e)=0\,\forall e\in F_{-},\ \omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}(e)=1\,\forall e\in F_{+}\big)&\text{ if }\Lambda=\mathbb{V}_{F},\\
\mathtt{ATRC}_{\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda}}^{0,1}\big(\psi^{-1}(\cdot)\big)&\text{ else }.\end{cases}

Note the small abuse of notation: we used the same notation as in (
25
) for the measure on sites rather than on edges. We are only interested in the first case, the second is only there to fit the setup of
[
Ott25
]
.

Mixing hypotheses.

The hypotheses Mix1 and Mix2 are exponential mixing hypotheses which are weak versions of the ratio weak mixing property (Theorem
3.3
).

Markov-type hypotheses.

To avoid lengthy displays, introduce the shorthand

F^{\prime}=\begin{cases}F&\text{ if }\mathbb{V}_{F}=\Lambda,\\
\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda}&\text{ else}.\end{cases}

Hypotheses Mar1, Mar2, Mar3 ask for

•

a family of local events,
\mathrm{Ma}_{i}
,
i\in\Gamma_{l}
, supported on
\mathbb{E}_{BL}([i]_{3l+1})
;

•

a family of local events,
\mathrm{Ma}_{i;\Lambda}^{t}
,
i\in\Gamma_{l}
,
t=(F,a,b,F_{-},F_{+})\in T
,
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, supported on
\mathbb{E}_{BL}([i]_{3l+1})\cap F^{\prime}
;

•

configurations
\mathrm{a}_{i}\in\{(0,0),(0,1),(1,1)\}^{\mathbb{E}_{BL}([i]_{l})}
,
i\in\Gamma_{l}
;

•

configurations
\mathrm{a}_{i,\Lambda}^{t}\in\{(0,0),(0,1),(1,1)\}^{\mathbb{E}_{BL}([i]_{l})\cap F^{\prime}}
,
i\in\Gamma_{l}
.

These should satisfy:

(1)

if
\gamma
is a simple closed nearest-neighbour path in
\Gamma_{l}
, and
(\xi,\xi^{\prime})
is a configuration on
F^{\prime}\cap\mathbb{E}_{[\gamma]_{3l+1}}
such that for any
i\in\gamma
with
\mathbb{E}_{[i]_{5l+2}}\subset F^{\prime}
one has

(\xi,\xi^{\prime})|_{\mathbb{E}_{[i]_{3l+1}}}\in\mathrm{Ma}_{i},

and for any
i\in\gamma
with
\mathbb{E}_{[i]_{5l+2}}\not\subset F^{\prime}
,
\mathbb{E}_{[i]_{3l+1}}\cap F^{\prime}\neq\varnothing
, one has

(\xi,\xi^{\prime})|_{\mathbb{E}_{[i]_{3l+1}}\cap F^{\prime}}\in\mathrm{Ma}_{i;\Lambda}^{t},

then
(\xi,\xi^{\prime})
contains a pair of decoupling paths for
\gamma
in
F^{\prime}
. This is Mar1.

(2)

The probability of
\mathrm{Ma}_{i}
conditionally on the configuration outside of
\mathbb{E}_{[i]_{3l+1}}
is lower bounded by
p_{0}<1
fixed universal, uniformly over the configuration outside of
\mathbb{E}_{[i]_{3l+1}}
. This is Mar2.

(3)

If
w\in\mathrm{Ma}_{i}
, and
j\in\Gamma_{l}
with
\norm{i-j}_{\infty}\leq 2l+1
, then the configuration obtained by swapping the value of
w|_{\mathbb{E}_{BL}([j]_{l})}
to
\mathrm{a}_{j}
is still in
\mathrm{Ma}_{i}
, and similarly for
\mathrm{a}_{i,\Lambda}^{t}
. Moreover, the probability of
\mathrm{a}_{i}
,
\mathrm{a}_{i,\Lambda}^{t}
conditionally on the configuration outside of their respective supports is greater than
\theta>0
for some
\theta>0
uniform over the the configuration outside of the support. This is Mar3.

Take the events/configurations (
l
is taken large enough):

•

\mathrm{Ma}_{i}
is the event that
\mathbb{E}_{[i]_{3l/2}}\setminus\mathbb{E}_{[i]_{l+1}}
contains both a simple closed path of edges open in
\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}
surrounding
[i]_{l+1}
, and a simple closed path of dual edges open in
\omega_{\tau}^{*}
surrounding
[i]_{l+1}
.

•

\mathrm{a}_{i,\Lambda}^{t}
, and
\mathrm{a}_{i}
are the constant
(0,1)
configurations.

•

\mathrm{Ma}_{i,\Lambda}^{t}
contains only the constant
(0,1)
configuration.

See Fig.
13
. Mar1 follows by Jordan’s curve Theorem. Mar2 follows (once
l
is fixed large enough) from exponential mixing (Theorem
5.15
) and uniform exponential decay of connectivities in
\omega_{\tau}
, and
\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}^{*}
(Theorem
3.5
). Finally, Mar3 follows from finite energy (
FE
).

Figure 13.
Left: a path in
\Gamma_{1}
(black disks), the blue edges have their state forced to be
(0,1)
, the black disks in a green square imply the realization of
\mathrm{Ma}_{i}
at that place. Right: a realization of
\mathrm{Ma}_{i}
(blue edges are open in
\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}
, while the dashed red path represent the dual of edges closed in
\omega_{\tau}
).

7.
Random Walk picture in the ATRC

We start this section by introducing the objects necessary to the development of the “Ornstein-Zernike theory” (renewal picture of connection probabilities). We follow the general strategy used in
[
CI02
,
CIV03
,
CIV08
,
OV18
,
AOV24
]
, most results will be imported when their proof is a repetition of existing arguments.
To keep notations readable, we will use the following short-hand throughout this section:

\Phi\equiv\mathtt{ATRC}_{J,U},\qquad\Phi_{\Lambda}^{a,b}\equiv\mathtt{ATRC}_{\Lambda;J,U}^{a,b},

where
\mathtt{ATRC}_{J,U}
is the unique infinite-volume measure for the
\mathtt{ATRC}
model (recall Proposition
5.12
).
The theorems that will be used in other sections will be stated with the notation matching the rest of the paper. Moreover, again to lighten notations,

\text{connections are in }\omega_{\tau}\text{ unless explicitly stated otherwise.}

Define
\mathcal{C}
as the cluster of
0
in
\omega_{\tau}
.

7.1.
Decay rate, Cones, and Diamonds

Start by introducing some objects.

Norm induced by the decay rates and associated sets.

For
s\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
, define

(27)

\nu(s)=-\lim_{n\to\infty}\tfrac{1}{n}\ln\Phi(0\leftrightarrow ns).

Existence of the limit follows in the standard fashion by Fekete’s Lemma, using sub-additivity which follows from the FKG inequality.
We now extend
\nu
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by positive homogeneity of order one: for any
s\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
and
r\geq 0
, define

\nu(rs):=r\nu(s).

Using existence of the limit and the FKG inequality, one can show that
\nu
is a non-degenerate norm as soon as it is non-zero:

\nu
is positive homogeneous by definition;
exponential decay of connection probabilities in
\Phi
(the ATRC model) implies positivity;
the FKG inequality for
\Phi
implies the triangular inequality for
\nu
. See
[
Ale01
, Section 2]
for details.
Clearly,
\nu
inherits the symmetries of the lattice, that is axial and diagonal reflections and rotations by
\pi/2
.
From these symmetry considerations, one has that for any
s\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
,

\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\nu(\mathrm{e}_{1})\leq\nu(s)\leq\sqrt{2}\nu(\mathrm{e}_{1}),

with
\mathrm{e}_{1}=(1,0)
. Moreover, one has

(28)

\Phi(0\leftrightarrow x)=e^{-\nu(x)(1+o(1))}.

As
\nu
is a norm, there are two convex sets naturally associated to it: the equi-decay set
\mathcal{U}
, and the convex set of which
\nu
is the support function,
\mathcal{W}
.

(29)

\mathcal{U}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\ \nu(x)\leq 1\},\qquad\mathcal{W}=\bigcap_{s\in\mathbb{S}^{1}}\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\ x\cdot s\leq\nu(s)\}.

It is easy to see that
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{W}
are dual to each other, that is:
\mathcal{W}
is the equi-decay set of the norm dual to
\nu
, and the norm dual to
\nu
is the support function of
\mathcal{U}
.
We say that
(s,t)\in\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\partial\mathcal{W}
is a
dual pair
if
\nu(s)=t\cdot s
.
From the definitions, one has, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(30)

\nu(x)=\max_{t\in\partial\mathcal{W}}t\cdot x.

We refer to
[
Roc70
]
for details on convex duality. From the last display, it is also easy to see that
\mathcal{W}
is the closure of the convergence domain of

(31)

\mathbb{G}(t)=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\Phi(0\leftrightarrow x)e^{t\cdot x}.

Cones and Diamonds.

Let
t\in\partial\mathcal{W},\delta\in(0,1)
. Let us introduce the geometric objects used in the interface study. We first define the cones and the associated diamonds (see Fig.
14
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleleft}_{t,\delta}:=\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\ x\cdot t\geq(1-\delta)\nu(x)\big\},\quad\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleright}_{t,\delta}:=-\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleleft}_{t,\delta},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Diamond}_{t,\delta}(u,v):=(u+\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleleft}_{t,\delta})\cap(v+\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleright}_{t,\delta}).

Figure 14.
Forward and backward cones, and the associated diamond.

As
\delta
goes to
1
, the cone
\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleleft}_{t,\delta}
converges to the half-plane that contains
t
and whose boundary is orthogonal to
t
. As
\delta
goes to
0
, the cone
\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleleft}_{t,\delta}
converges to the convex cone generated by the directions dual to
t
.
The latter set is a line when
\nu
is strictly convex.

Let
V\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. We will say that
V
is:

•

(t,\delta)
-
forward-confined
if there exists
u\in V
such that
V\subset u+\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleleft}_{t,\delta}
. When it exists, such a
u
is unique; we denote it by
\mathbf{f}({V})
.

•

(t,\delta)
-
backward-confined
if there exists
v\in V
such that
V\subset v+\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleright}_{t,\delta}
. When it exists, such a
v
is unique; we denote it by
\mathbf{b}({V})
.

•

(t,\delta)
-
diamond-confined
if it is both forward- and backward-confined.

We will say that
v\in V
is a
(t,\delta)
-cone-point
of
V
if

V\subset v+(\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleright}_{t,\delta}\cup\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleleft}_{t,\delta}).

Define
\textnormal{CPts}_{t,\delta}(V)
as the set of cone-points of
V
. When speaking about cone-points of graphs, we mean cone-points of their vertex set.

We call a graph with a distinguished vertex a
marked graph
. The distinguished vertex is denoted
v^{*}
. Define the following objects (see Fig.
15
):

•

The sets of confined pieces are the following sets of finite connected subgraphs of
(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\mathbb{E})
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{L}(t,\delta)

\displaystyle=\{(\gamma,0)\text{ marked backward-confined }\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{R}(t,\delta)

\displaystyle=\{(\gamma,v^{*})\text{ marked forward-confined with }\mathbf{f}(\gamma)=0\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}(t,\delta)

\displaystyle=\{\gamma\text{ diamond-confined with }\mathbf{f}(\gamma)=0\}.

To fix ideas we shall, unless stated otherwise, think of
\mathfrak{A}
as of a subset of
\mathfrak{B}_{L}
, that is, by default the vertex
\mathbf{f}(\gamma)=0
is marked for any
\gamma\in\mathfrak{A}
.
Note that
\mathfrak{A}
can alternatively be viewed as subset of
\mathfrak{B}_{R}
by marking
\mathbf{b}(\gamma)
. We will usually omit the marked vertex from the notation as it will be clear from the context.

•

The displacement along a piece:

(32)

\mathrm{X}(\gamma):=\begin{cases}\mathbf{b}(\gamma)&\text{ if }\gamma\in\mathfrak{B}_{L},\ \text{in particular, if $\gamma\in\mathfrak{A}$,}\\
v^{*}&\text{ if }\gamma\in\mathfrak{B}_{R}.\end{cases}

•

The
concatenation
operation (see Fig.
16
): for
\gamma_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}_{L}
and
\gamma_{2}\in\mathfrak{B}_{R}
define the concatenation of
\gamma_{2}
to
\gamma_{1}
as

\gamma_{1}\circ\gamma_{2}=\gamma_{1}\cup(\mathrm{X}(\gamma_{1})+\gamma_{2}).

The concatenation of two graphs in
\mathfrak{A}
is an element of
\mathfrak{A}
, the concatenation of
a graph in
\mathfrak{A}
to an element of
\mathfrak{B}_{L}
is an element of
\mathfrak{B}_{L}
, and the concatenation of a element in
\mathfrak{B}_{R}
to an graph in
\mathfrak{A}
is an element of
\mathfrak{B}_{R}
. The displacement along a concatenation is the sum of the displacements along the pieces.

Figure 15.
Backward, forward, and diamond confined marked graphs,with their displacement.

Figure 16.
Concatenation of confined graphs. Red dots are the marked vertices of the marked graphs.

These sets can be seen as equivalence classes of general marked forward, backward, or diamond confined graphs modulo translations. A general such graph can then be recovered uniquely from an element of
\mathfrak{B}_{L}
,
\mathfrak{B}_{R}
, or
\mathfrak{A}
by specifying the translation vector.

7.2.
Main result of the section

Our main goal is to prove the following “coupling with random walk” in infinite volume for long connections in the ATRC model.
Recall that
\mathrm{e}_{1}=(1,0),\mathrm{e}_{2}=(0,1)
are the canonical basis vectors and
\mathcal{C}
denotes the cluster of
0
in
\omega_{\tau}
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
0\leq J<U
be such that
\sinh(2J)=e^{-2U}
. Let
s_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
,
t\in\partial\mathcal{W}
be dual to
s_{0}
and
\delta\in(0,1)
be such that the interior of
\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleleft}_{t,\delta}
contains an element of
\{\pm\mathrm{e}_{1},\pm\mathrm{e}_{2}\}
. Then, there exist constants
\mathrm{C},C_{1},C_{2}\geq 0,c_{1},c_{2}>0,\epsilon>0
and probability measures
p_{L}
,
p_{R}
, and
p
on
\mathfrak{B}_{L}(t,\delta)
,
\mathfrak{B}_{R}(t,\delta)
, and
\mathfrak{A}(t,\delta)
respectively such that

(1)

renewal structure:
for any
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that
x\cdot s_{0}\geq(1-\epsilon)|x|
, and any
f
real valued function of the cluster of
0
,

(33)

\Big|\mathrm{C}\sum_{\gamma_{L},\gamma_{R}}p_{L}(\gamma_{L})p_{R}(\gamma_{R})\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k}}f(\bar{\gamma})\mathds{1}_{\mathrm{X}(\bar{\gamma})=x}\prod_{i=1}^{k}p(\gamma_{k})\\
-e^{t\cdot x}\mathtt{ATRC}_{J,U}\big(f(\mathcal{C})\mathds{1}_{0\xleftrightarrow{\omega_{\tau}}x}\big)\Big|\leq C_{1}\norm{f}_{\infty}e^{-c_{1}|x|},

where
\bar{\gamma}=\gamma_{L}\circ\gamma_{1}\circ\dots\circ\gamma_{k}\circ\gamma_{R}
, and the sums are over
\gamma_{L}\in\mathfrak{B}_{L}(t,\delta)
,
\gamma_{R}\in\mathfrak{B}_{R}(t,\delta)
, and
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k}\in\mathfrak{A}(t,\delta)
;

(2)

exponential tails and “finite energy” for the steps:
for
(p_{*},D_{*})\in\{(p_{L},\mathfrak{B}_{L}(t,\delta)),(p_{R},\mathfrak{B}_{R}(t,\delta)),(p,\mathfrak{A}(t,\delta))\}
,

(34)

\sum_{\gamma\in D_{*}}p_{*}(\gamma)\mathds{1}_{\norm{\displace(\gamma)}\geq\ell}\leq C_{2}e^{-c_{2}\ell}\quad\forall\ell\geq 0,

(35)

p_{*}\big(\gamma\big)\geq\alpha^{|\gamma|+1}\quad\forall\gamma\in D_{*}:\ \textnormal{CPts}_{t,\delta}(\gamma)\cap\mathrm{Diamond}_{t,\delta}(0,\mathrm{X}(\gamma))\subset\{0,\mathrm{X}(\gamma)\},

for some
\alpha>0
depending on
J,U
only;

(3)

mean value of a step is proportional to
s_{0}
:
there exists
a>0
such that

(36)

\sum_{\gamma\in\mathfrak{A}(t,\delta)}p(\gamma)\mathrm{X}(\gamma)=as_{0}.

Moreover,
\partial\mathcal{U}
,
\partial\mathcal{W}
are analytic manifolds and the norm
\nu
is uniformly strictly convex, that is
\mathcal{U}
is strictly convex and
\partial\mathcal{U}
has uniformly lower bounded curvature.
In particular, each direction
s\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
has a unique dual vector
t_{s}\in\partial\mathcal{W}
and there exist
\kappa>0
such that the following sharp triangle inequality holds:

\nu(x)+\nu(y)-\nu(x+y)\geq\kappa(|x|+|y|-|x+y|).

The rest of the section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
7.1
. Subsections
7.3
,
7.4
, and
7.5
are preparations for the core of the proof. The proof itself is then divided into two main steps, presented respectively in Subsections
7.6
and
7.7
.

Remark 7.2
.

We use the value of
J,U
through only two inputs: the exponential decay in
\omega_{\tau}
and
\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}^{*}
, and the mixing of Theorems
3.3
and
6.1
(which follows from edge relaxation, Proposition
3.2
, and exponential decay in
\omega_{\tau}
and
\omega_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}^{*}
). In particular, if one can extend these properties beyond the self-dual line (part of which is done in
[
ADG24
]
), Theorem
7.1
extends directly.

7.3.
The coarse-graining procedure

The first step is to analyse the typical geometry of long connections. The analysis of
[
CIV08
,
AOV24
]
is based on a coarse-graining of the cluster of
0
.
Recall the equi-decay set
\mathcal{U}
defined in (
29
).
Introduce the cells: for
K,k\geq 1
and
A\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,

\displaystyle[A]_{k}=\bigcup_{x\in A}(x+\{-k,\dots,k\}^{2}),

\displaystyle\Delta_{K}=K\mathcal{U}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{2},\quad\Delta_{K}^{\prime}=[\Delta_{K}]_{\ln(K)^{2}}.

The scale parameter
K
will be picked large enough in the course of the proof.

We then coarse-grain the cluster of
0
using the same algorithm as in
[
CIV08
]
. Denote
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{K}
and
\Delta^{\prime}\equiv\Delta_{K}^{\prime}
. For
C
a realization of
\mathcal{C}
define
\mathrm{Sk}(C)
via the next algorithm (see also Fig.
17
).

Set
v_{0}=0
,
\mathrm{Sk}_{V}=\{v_{0}\}
,
\mathrm{Sk}_{E}=\varnothing
,
V=\Delta^{\prime}
,
i=1
;

while

A=\big\{z\in\partial^{\mathrm{ex}}V:\ z\xleftrightarrow{(z+\Delta)\setminus V}\partial^{\mathrm{in}}(z+\Delta)\big\}\neq\varnothing

do

Set
v_{i}=\min A
;

Let
v^{*}
be the smallest
v\in\mathrm{Sk}_{V}
such that
v_{i}\in\partial^{\mathrm{in}}(\Delta+v^{*})
;

Update
\mathrm{Sk}_{V}=\mathrm{Sk}_{V}\cup\{v_{i}\}
,
\mathrm{Sk}_{E}=\mathrm{Sk}_{E}\cup\{\{v^{*},v_{i}\}\}
,
V=V\cup(v_{i}+\Delta^{\prime})
,
i=i+1
;

end while

return

(\mathrm{Sk}_{V},\mathrm{Sk}_{E})
;

Algorithm 1

Coarse graining of a cluster containing
0
.

The output
\mathrm{Sk}(C)=(\mathrm{Sk}_{V}(C),\mathrm{Sk}_{E}(C))
of Algorithm
1
is a tree with
\mathrm{Sk}_{V}(C)\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
; we use the shorthand
|\mathrm{Sk}(C)|:=|\mathrm{Sk}_{V}(C)|
for its size.

Figure 17.
From top to bottom: a realization of
\mathcal{C}
(
0
is in red); the cells of the associated coarse-graining; the connections implied by the presence of the cells; the skeleton (in black).

7.4.
Energy-Entropy estimates

The next step is to establish energy bounds which control the probability to see a given tree as the skeleton of a cluster.

Lemma 7.3
.

For any
0\in A\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
K\geq 1
,

(37)

\Phi_{[\Delta_{K}\cap A]_{\ln(K)^{2}}}^{1,1}(0\xleftrightarrow{\Delta_{K}\cap A}\partial^{\mathrm{in}}\Delta_{K})\leq e^{-K(1+o_{K}(1))},

where
o_{K}(1)
is a quantity that goes to
0
as
K
goes to
\infty
.

Note that the wanted probabilities are zero whenever
A\cap\Delta_{K}
does not contain a path going from
0
to
\partial^{\mathrm{in}}\Delta_{K}
.

Proof.

By the definition of
\mathcal{U}
, the ratio weak mixing property (Theorem
3.3
) and monotonicity:

\displaystyle\Phi_{[\Delta_{K}\cap A]_{\ln(K)^{2}}}^{1,1}(0\xleftrightarrow{\Delta_{K}\cap A}\partial^{\mathrm{in}}\Delta_{K})

\displaystyle\leq(1+CK^{2}e^{-c^{\prime}\ln(K)^{2}})\Phi_{[\Delta_{K}\cap A]_{\ln(K)^{2}}}^{0,0}(0\xleftrightarrow{\Delta_{K}\cap A}\partial^{\mathrm{in}}\Delta_{K})

\displaystyle\leq 2\Phi(0\xleftrightarrow{\Delta_{K}}\partial^{\mathrm{in}}\Delta_{K})

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{x\in\partial^{\mathrm{in}}\Delta_{K}}e^{-\nu(x)(1+o_{K}(1))}=C|\partial^{\mathrm{in}}\Delta_{K}|e^{-K(1+o_{K}(1))}

as soon as
K
is large enough.
∎

As a direct consequence of Lemma
7.3
and of the definition of the coarse-graining procedure, one gets the following:

Lemma 7.4

(Energy bound)
.

There exists
K_{0}\geq 0
such that, for any
K\geq K_{0}
,
and any
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,

\Phi\big(\mathrm{Sk}_{V}(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{T}\big)\leq e^{-K|\mathcal{T}|(1+o_{K}(1))},

where the
o_{K}
is uniform over
\mathcal{T}

Indeed, every new vertex of the tree away from the boundary induces a connection of the form (
37
) in the complement of the neighbourhood of the previously explored vertices (See Fig.
17
). We do not provide further details,
see for example
[
CIV08
, Display (2.2)]
for the implementation of the bound.

Recall that
\mathrm{Sk}(C)
is a tree rooted at
0
.
Denote by
\mathrm{Tree}_{N}
the set of all possible values of
\mathrm{Sk}(C)
if
|\mathrm{Sk}(C)|=N
.
We now state a general combinatorial lemma that bounds the size of
\mathrm{Tree}_{N}
.

Lemma 7.5

(Entropy bound)
.

There exists a universal
c>0
such that

|\mathrm{Tree}_{N}|\leq e^{c\ln(K)N}=K^{cN}.

This Lemma follows from the fact that, for some
C\geq 0
, the size of
\mathrm{Tree}_{N}
is smaller or equal to the number of
N
-vertex connected sub-trees of the
CK^{2}
-regular tree containing
0
.
The latter is bounded by
e^{c\ln(K)N}
for some
c>0
by Kesten’s argument
[
Kes82
, page 85]
.

7.5.
Input from
[
CIV08
,
CIV03
]
: skeleton and cluster cone-points

The main result that we import from
[
CIV08
,
CIV03
]
is
[
CIV08
, Theorem 2.1]
that describes a typical geometry of skeletons (
[
CIV08
]
builds on
[
CIV03
]
).
The result in
[
CIV08
]
is stated for the FK-percolation, but the proof is general and relies only on Lemmata
7.4
and
7.5
.

Lemma 7.6

(Cone-points of a skeleton)
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},P)
be a probability space,
v\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, and let
\mathcal{C}
be a random finite connected subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
containing
v
defined on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},P)
. Suppose that for any tree
\mathcal{T}
in the image of
\mathrm{Sk}
,
K\geq 0
,

P(\mathrm{Sk}(\mathcal{C}-v)=\mathcal{T})\leq e^{-K|\mathcal{T}|(1+o_{K}(1))}.

Then, for any
\delta>0
, there exist
c_{1},c_{2}>0
,
K_{0}\geq 0
such that, for any
K\geq K_{0}
,
t\in\partial\mathcal{W}
,
w\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,

e^{t\cdot(w-v)}P\big(w\in\mathcal{C},|\textnormal{CPts}_{t,\delta}(\mathrm{Sk}(\mathcal{C}-v))|\leq c_{1}|w-v|/K\big)\leq e^{-c_{2}|w-v|}.

Moreover, by monotonicity,
c_{1},c_{2},K_{0}
can be taken uniform over
\delta\geq\delta_{0}>0
.

Remark 7.7
.

Note that the Lemma holds directly when
w
is not in
v+\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleleft}_{t,\delta}
: then
P(w\in\mathcal{C})\leq e^{-\nu(w-v)(1+o(1))}
(as the event
w\in\mathcal{C}
implies that
|\mathrm{Sk}(\mathcal{C})|\geq\nu(w-v)/K
) but
t\cdot(w-v)-\nu(w-v)\leq-\delta\nu(w-v)
by definition of
\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleleft}_{t,\delta}
.

The second result we import is a simple but notationally heavy use of finite energy. One can find two different implementations of this argument in
[
CIV08
, Section 2.9]
, and
[
AOV24
, Section 6.1]
. Introduce a small variation on the notion of cone-points which will be convenient later (one could work directly with cone-points, but the equations become a bit heavier).
Recall
\mathrm{e}_{1}=(1,0)
,
\mathrm{e}_{2}=(0,1)
.

Definition 7.8

(Regular Cone-points)
.

Let
C=(V,E)
be a connected subgraph of
(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\mathbb{E})
. Say that
v\in V
is a
regular
(t,\delta)
-cone-point
of
C
(see Fig.
18
) if it is a
(t,\delta)
-cone-point of
V
and, if
\{\pm\mathrm{e}_{1}\}\cap\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleleft}_{t,\delta}\neq\varnothing
,
\{v,v+\mathrm{e}_{1}\},\{v,v-\mathrm{e}_{1}\}\in E
and
\{v,v+\mathrm{e}_{2}\},\{v,v-\mathrm{e}_{2}\}\notin E
.
Define
\mathrm{rCPts}_{t,\delta}(C)
as the set of
(t,\delta)
-regular cone-points of
C
.

Note than when
\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleleft}_{t,\delta}
contains exactly one element of
\{\pm\mathrm{e}_{1},\pm\mathrm{e}_{2}\}
, all cone-points are necessarily regular.

Figure 18.
Left: a cone-point that is
not
regular. Right: a regular cone-point.

The idea for going from Lemma
7.6
to the next lemma is simple: when
v\leftrightarrow w
, up to an exponentially small error, there must be at least
c|v-w|/K
cone-points of the skeleton by Lemma
7.6
.
Up to anther exponentially small error, a positive fraction of these cone-points must then be regular cone-points of
\mathcal{C}
by uniform finite energy.

Lemma 7.9

(Cluster Cone-points)
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},P)
be probability space,
v\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, and
\omega:\Omega\to\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{E}}
be a bond percolation random variable.
The cluster of
v
in
\omega
is denoted by
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}(\omega)
.
Suppose that

•

\omega
has uniform finite energy (for opening and closing edges): for
e\in\mathbb{E}
, let
\mathcal{F}_{e^{c}}
be the sigma-algebra generated by
(\omega_{f})_{f\neq e}
. Then, there exists
\epsilon\in(0,1)
such that for every
e\in\mathbb{E}
,

\epsilon<E(\omega_{e}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{e^{c}})<1-\epsilon,

P
-almost surely.

•

the conclusion of Lemma
7.6
holds for
\mathcal{C}
.

Then, for any
\delta>0
, there exist
c_{1}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}>0
,
L_{0}\geq 1
such that, for any
t\in\partial\mathcal{W}
such that the interior of
\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleleft}_{t,\delta}
contains an element of
\{\pm\mathrm{e}_{1},\pm\mathrm{e}_{2}\}
, and any

w\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\cap\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleleft}_{t,\delta}
with
|w-v|\geq L_{0}
,

e^{t\cdot(w-v)}P\big(w\in\mathcal{C},\ |\mathrm{rCPts}_{t,\delta}(\mathcal{C})|\leq c_{1}^{\prime}|w-v|\big)\leq e^{-c_{2}^{\prime}|v-w|}.

By monotonicity,
c_{1}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime},L_{0}
can be taken uniform over
\delta\geq\delta_{0}>0
.

See
[
CIV08
, Section 2.9]
or
[
AOV24
, Section 6.1]
for two different proofs of Lemma
7.9
via local surgery arguments.
From these two lemmas we can deduce our main cone-point estimate.

Theorem 7.10
.

Let
\delta\in(0,1)
. There exist
C\geq 0,c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that for any
t\in\partial\mathcal{W}
such that the interior of
\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleleft}_{t,\delta}
contains an element of
\{\pm\mathrm{e}_{1},\pm\mathrm{e}_{2}\}
, and any
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,

e^{t\cdot x}\Phi\big(0\leftrightarrow x,|\mathrm{rCPts}_{t,\delta}(\mathcal{C})|\leq c_{1}|x|\big)\leq Ce^{-c_{2}|x|}.

Proof.

When
x\notin\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleleft}_{t,\delta}
, the claim is trivial, see Remark
7.7
. For
x\in\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleleft}_{t,\delta}
, the result follows from Lemmata
7.6
, and
7.9
: Lemma
7.4
provides the required bound on the probability of a given skeleton, and the model has finite energy, which are the needed hypotheses for Lemmata
7.6
, and
7.9
.
∎

This concludes our preparations. We are now ready to attack the proof of Theorem
7.1
.

7.6.
Proof of Theorem
7.1
part I: Cone-points and pre-renewal structure

We now fix
s_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
,
t_{0}\in\partial\mathcal{W}
dual to
s_{0}
,
\delta\in(0,1)
such that the interior of
\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleleft}_{t_{0},\delta}
contains and element of
\{\pm\mathrm{e}_{1},\pm\mathrm{e}_{2}\}
, and we set

(38)

\begin{gathered}\mathrm{rCPts}\equiv\mathrm{rCPts}_{t_{0},\delta},\quad\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleleft}\equiv\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleleft}_{t_{0},\delta},\quad\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleright}\equiv\mathcal{Y}^{\blacktriangleright}_{t_{0},\delta},\\
\mathfrak{B}_{L}\equiv\mathfrak{B}_{L}(t_{0},\delta),\quad\mathfrak{B}_{R}\equiv\mathfrak{B}_{R}(t_{0},\delta),\quad\mathfrak{A}\equiv\mathfrak{A}(t_{0},\delta).\end{gathered}

From Theorem
7.10
, typical long clusters have many cone-points: having a non-linear (in
|x|
), number of cone-points is exponentially more unlikely than
\{x\in\mathcal{C}\}
. The idea is now to write a realization of
\mathcal{C}
